● Confirmed defect, and exactly the dangerous one. On Ctrl-C, rclpy tears down
  the ROS context before my handler runs, so the "land where you are" command
  cannot be sent — the drones would keep flying their last figure. The code
  reported the failure honestly, but the feature didn't work.

  The fix is to take the signal myself after Crazyswarm() has initialised, so
  the context is still valid when the landing is commanded:

● This one belongs in Gotchas — it will bite any future script that tries to
  land on Ctrl-C:

● Update(CLAUDE.md)
  ⎿  Added 14 lines
      246    complete and parameter service calls time out; (3) `Crazyswa
           rm()` in a fresh
      247    rclpy process can hang waiting on `all/emergency` for the sa
           me reason
      248    (`color_led.py` avoids Crazyswarm() and calls `set_parameter
           s` directly).
      249 +- **Ctrl-C kills the ROS context BEFORE your handler runs — so
          + "land on abort"                                              
      250 +  silently does nothing.** `rclpy.init` (inside `Crazyswarm()`
          +) installs its own                                            
      251 +  SIGINT handler that shuts the context down; by the time `Key
          +boardInterrupt`                                               
      252 +  reaches the script, `allcfs.land()` dies with `failed to ini
          +tialize wait set:                                             
      253 +  the given context is not valid` — at exactly the moment it i
          +s needed, leaving                                             
      254 +  the drones flying their last command. Verified failing in si
          +m 2026-09-20.                                                 
      255 +  Fix: after `Crazyswarm()` returns, take the signal back with
      256 +  `signal.signal(signal.SIGINT, ...)` (and SIGTERM, which the 
          +console's Stop                                                
      257 +  sends) and raise your own exception — see `take_signals()` i
          +n                                                             
      258 +  `crazyflie_shows/constellation_show.py`. Restore `SIG_DFL` o
          +nce the abort                                                 
      259 +  starts so a second Ctrl-C can still kill the process. Note `
          +ros2 run` does                                                
      260 +  NOT forward a signal sent to it alone; a terminal Ctrl-C rea
          +ches the child                                                
      261 +  because it goes to the whole foreground process group, so te
          +st an abort with                                              
      262 +  `kill -INT <the script's own PID>`, not the wrapper's.      
      263  - **Server BLOCKS FOREVER on the first unreachable enabled dro
           ne.** The cpp
      264    server connects drones in lexicographic `std::map` order and
            hangs
      265    **silently** on the first enabled drone that doesn't answer 
           radio — one

● The new show is done and the abort defect is fixed.

  The abort bug, which was real. On Ctrl-C, rclpy's own SIGINT handler
  (installed inside Crazyswarm()) tore down the ROS context before my code ran,
  so allcfs.land() failed with "the given context is not valid" — the drones
  would have kept flying their last figure. Fixed by taking SIGINT/SIGTERM back
  after Crazyswarm() initialises (take_signals(), constellation_show.py:63).
  Re-tested in sim, interrupted mid-turbine at 0.6–1.45 m: landed and disarmed,
  all five at z ≈ 0 in /tf, clean exit 130. A second Ctrl-C now kills the
  process outright so you can always give up on the script.

  One thing the failed test taught me: ros2 run swallows a signal sent to it
  alone. My first re-test looked like the fix had done nothing, when in fact I'd
  signalled the wrapper. A terminal Ctrl-C hits the whole process group, so
  your Ctrl-C works — but kill -INT needs the script's own PID. That's now in
  CLAUDE.md's Gotchas, since it will mislead the next person too.

  The show — "constellation" (constellation_show / plan_constellation), 78 s =
  39 bars at 120 BPM:

  pentagon → swashplate → arrow → dart/recoil → pyramid → turbine → spiral 
  staircase → back down the same path → starburst finale → home.

  Three things make it different from your carousel show, all measured rather
  than chosen:
  - Plan-view clearance is the enforced invariant — 0.99 m against the 0.90 m
    floor, and no drone is ever above another. Height became decorative, which
    is why this show has 1.00 m of 3D separation where the carousel has 0.91 m.
  - The shape order is an arch because nothing else verified. Against your real
    start marks, no one-way route through the four shapes keeps clearance; the
    staircase can only be entered from and left to the pyramid. So the second
    half retraces the first — which also makes the reversed turbine free in
    trajectory memory (25/31 pieces total).
  - Slots are assigned by bottleneck distance, never by drone name, so a
    position re-sync re-solves the whole show.

  Sim: landed at t+78.0 s, 0 late phases, flown separation 0.995 m vs 0.997 m
  planned (2 mm loss, vs 49 mm for the counterflow figure).

  Docs are in src/crazyswarm2/crazyflie_shows/CONSTELLATION.md, including the
  hardware checklist — the one thing I'd press on is flying it first at bpm:=100
  (≈94 s, identical geometry, more reaction time). The console already lists
  both new entry points, no edit needed.

  Sim stack and GUIs are all stopped; nothing left running. Nothing is committed
  — say the word and I'll commit and push to origin.

      235 +def staircase(center, n, spacing, low, high, heading_deg, stag
          +ger=0.0):                                                     
      236 +    """Steps rising from ``low`` to ``high``, switching back a
          +s they climb.                                                 
      237 +                                                              
      238 +    ``stagger`` offsets alternate steps sideways (peak to peak
          +), which is what                                              
      239 +    lets this shape fit the room. A straight line of ``n`` dro
          +nes must be                                                   
      240 +    ``(n-1) x PLAN_SEPARATION`` long -- 3.6 m at five drones, 
          +so +-1.8 m of                                                 
      241 +    radius spent on one figure, and it grows with every drone 
          +added. Offsetting                                             
      242 +    alternate steps puts the clearance on the diagonal instead
          +: adjacent steps                                              
      243 +    are ``hypot(spacing, stagger)`` apart, so ``spacing`` can 
          +be well under the                                             
      244 +    separation floor and the footprint shrinks with it. MEASUR
          +ED, 2026-10-02:                                               
      245 +    worst footprint over n=4..6 falls from 2.62 m to 1.43 m.  
      246 +                                                              
      247 +    It also reads better -- a switchback stair instead of a ra
          +mp seen edge-on.                                              
      248 +    """                                                       
      249 +    offs = [((i - (n - 1) / 2.0) * spacing,                   
      250 +             (stagger / 2.0) * (1.0 if i % 2 else -1.0)) for i
          + in range(n)]                                                 
      251      zs = list(np.linspace(low, high, n))
      252      return _place(center, offs, zs, heading_deg)
      253

    centroid. It now measures from ARENA_CENTRE with a 1.90 m budget (2.00 m
    tested − 0.10 m tracking margin), plus a cone ceiling, and the show builds
    its figures at the volume centre instead of wherever the drones are parked.
  - The staircase was the figure reaching the wall — a straight line costs
    (n−1)×0.90 m, so ±1.80 m at five drones. It now switches back (alternate
    steps offset 0.70 m sideways), putting clearance on the diagonal: worst
    footprint over n=4…6 fell from 2.62 m to 1.43 m. Dart shortened 0.60→0.50 m.

    re-checked, still passes (1.52 m).

● Nothing of mine is running and nothing has been armed. Here's where things
  stand.

  Your yaml question, answered by measurement rather than opinion: the
  duplication was my own crazyflie_shows package, not upstream and not
  AI-DA-STC. Upstream has one yaml and already exposes crazyflies_yaml_file as a
  launch argument. I deleted both copies — and it had already bitten you: the
  show's copy had no cf8 while the workspace's did, so the planner was checking
  one fleet while the server flew another.

  On the sync script — you were right that it stopped making sense, and the fix 
  was to change its scope, not its path. It now syncs every drone mocap is 
  streaming, enabled or not. A mark is a fact about where a drone is standing;
  it doesn't stop being true because that drone sat out a show. So: place once,
  sync once, then enable whichever subset the show needs — switching between the
  4-drone escort and the 5-drone constellation can no longer carry a stale
  mark. --enabled-only restores the old behaviour. I tested it with a synthetic
  /poses publisher (no hardware): parked drones get written and tagged, an
  enabled drone that isn't streamed is still a refusal (exit 1), and a parked
  drone standing <1 m from a flying one is reported as a mark that can't be used
  yet. That last message fired on your real data — cf4 is 0.89 m from cf5.

  For the override case nothing can detect, show_launch.py now prints a FLEET 
  YAML OVERRIDE banner naming the file and the exact --yaml sync command.
  Verified silent on the default, loud on an override.

  Two things I found about your actual fleet, from scanning (radio only — no 
  motors):
  - cf8 is silent on 0 of 6 passes while cf1–cf5 all answered, so I disabled it
    and enabled cf4 to match your cf1–cf5 setup. One edit, one file.
  - I owe you a correction. My first scan showed cf1/cf2/cf4 answering only at
    1M and I flagged a datarate mismatch against the yaml's 2M. That was wrong —
    the scan is simply intermittent. Over three passes at 2M: cf5 3/3, cf1 2/3,
    cf2/cf3/cf4 1/3. All five are reachable at the yaml's datarate; worth
    knowing that a single clean scan is not proof, and a single miss is not a
    dead drone.

  plan_constellation currently refuses, correctly: cf4's recorded mark is 0.89 m
  from cf5's. I also fixed its advice, which was telling you to grow the
  formation when the real fix is to move a drone and re-sync.

  Your next step, when you want it — I added server:=False so the state the sync
  script documents is now one command, with no radio owner and nothing armable:

  ros2 launch crazyflie launch.py server:=False      # mocap + GUIs, no server
  python3 scripts/sync_initial_positions.py --dry-run

  Say the word and I'll run the sync and the planner against the live marks.
  Nothing is committed yet.

  Why nothing flew: it was still uploading. Your output's last lines show
  figures 1, 2 and 3 done and the 4th in progress; the traceback is
  KeyboardInterrupt inside uploadTrajectory, i.e. the console's SIGINT landing
  on healthy work. I measured it: 54.6 s for 4 figures × 5 drones over one
  radio. You stopped at roughly 75%. Nothing was armed, and I wasn't holding any
  port — nothing of mine was running, and 1510/1511 were free.

  I've made the show stop looking dead there: it now prints each drone as it
  completes, states the expected duration up front, and says plainly that
  nothing is armed until "SHOW START" and that interrupting means re-uploading
  from scratch.

  The blocker you'd have hit 20 s later: all five drones report supervisor_info 
  = 576 — IS_LOCKED set, CAN_BE_ARMED clear. In the firmware,
  transitionsLocked[] has a single transition, back to itself, with blocker
  supervisorAlways — the locked state is terminal and only a battery power cycle
  clears it. One of its triggers is SUPERVISOR_CB_EMERGENCY_STOP, which matches
  the e-stop history on this rig. Batteries are fine (4.10–4.21 V) and nothing
  is tumbled or crashed.

  So: unplug and replug each of the five batteries, then I'll re-read the flags
  — a fresh drone should show canBeArmed yes, LOCKED -. To be straight about the
  evidence: I did not try arming (I won't without you asking), so this is read
  from the flags plus the firmware state machine, not an observed arm failure.

  Two other things worth knowing from your run:

  1. Your radius is at 93% of budget (1.76 m of 1.90 m), and it's not the 
     choreography — it's cf2's parked mark. The figures peak at 1.53 m; takeoff,
     gather, return and land over cf2 are the widest points of the whole show.
     Moving cf2 ~0.3 m toward the centre and re-syncing buys that margin back.
     Not a blocker, but it's the thing standing closest to the untracked edge.

     isn't faster.

● Your cflib log explains the inconsistency completely, and it isn't flaky —
  it's deterministic.

  Why the cflib launch gave you no topics: cf1's URI was radio://*/80/2M/... —
  the wildcard dongle. crazyflie-link-cpp resolves *; cflib's radiodriver.py
  does get_serials().index('*') → ValueError: Cannot find radio with serial *.
  cf1 fails, then the Python server prints "one or more Crazyflies can not be
  found" and exits (process has finished cleanly). No server → no /cfX/pose, no
  /cfX/status, no batteries. I pinned cf1 to radio://0/ — all six URIs now name
  dongle 0, and the yaml records why.

  The mocap exit code -6 in that log is not a crash during the run: it comes
  after user interrupted with ctrl-c, as receive_from: Interrupted system call.
  The node is blocked in recv, boost throws on SIGINT, terminate → SIGABRT.
  Documented, noisy, harmless at shutdown. During the run it was fine — "5 rigid
  bodies streamed".

  cflib vs cpp, from the code rather than memory:

  server
  cpp (default): C++ crazyflie_server.cpp, 1760 lines, crazyflie-link-cpp
  cflib: Python crazyflie_server.py, 1332 lines, Bitcraze cflib
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  broadcasts
  cpp (default): 76 references — /all/* and cmd_full_state go out as one packet
  cflib: 3 references
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  radio://* wildcard
  cpp (default): works
  cflib: crashes
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  one drone unreachable
  cpp (default): blocks silently on it
  cflib: exits the whole server
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  rig customizations
  cpp (default): LED green-on-connect, the add_on_set_parameters_callback fix so

  ros2 param set reaches drones, query_all_values_on_connect
  cflib: none of them

  I checked one assumption and it was wrong: the Python server has more
  custom-log support (44 refs vs 11), so kalman_preflight isn't a
  differentiator. Bottom line: use cpp — it's the default, broadcast-efficient
  with five drones on one radio, and everything on this rig is verified against
  it. cflib is for cflib-specific debugging.

  Good news: your power cycle worked. cf2, cf3, cf4 now report canBeArmed yes,
  LOCKED -.

  But there's a new, real blocker: your radio link is degraded across the whole 
  fleet. With cpp all five connect with zero disconnects, yet each logged ~100
  high-latency warnings in 35 s, cf3 lost 32 of 137 unicast packets, latencies
  ran 17–42 ms, and cf1 and cf5 deliver no /status or /pose at all (cf1 rssi 78
  vs cf4's 52). That's why you could read only some batteries. The show's
  rehearsal correctly refuses this.

  Two 2.4 GHz transmitters sit inches from the dongle, and I'd try them before
  suspecting the drones:

  1. Wi-Fi is associated to "AI.R STC Hangar" at 2427 MHz. You're on ethernet
     for Motive (192.168.9.109), so Wi-Fi is only giving you internet
     (192.168.10.196) — turning it off during flights costs you nothing on the
     rig.
  2. Bluetooth is enabled (rfkill: not blocked). It hops across 2402–2480 MHz,
     straight over your channel 80 (2480 MHz).
  3. The dongle (a Crazyradio 2.0, on its own root port) is in the laptop body
     next to both. A short USB extension, raised with line of sight, usually
     helps more than anything else.

  I didn't touch your Wi-Fi or Bluetooth — that's your machine's connectivity to
  decide. Toggle them off and I'll re-measure latency and packet loss the same
  way, so we know whether it's interference or the airframes.

  My stack is stopped; the radio and UDP 1511 are free. One stale thing I
  noticed and haven't fixed: the placement-check failure message still points at
  the obsolete 141.23.110.162 address — I'll clean that up next.

● Your instinct is right, and here's the arithmetic. The pose broadcast packs 2 
  drones per packet (if (j == 2)), position-only packs 4. With Motive at 50 Hz
  and this rig needing full poses (it force-fuses orientation):

  ┌─────────────────────────┬─────────────────┬─────────────────────┬───────┐
  │                         │ broadcast pkt/s │ unicast telemetry   │ total │
  │                         │                 │        pkt/s        │       │
  ├─────────────────────────┼─────────────────┼─────────────────────┼───────┤
  │ 4 drones (the last few  │ 2/frame × 50 =  │ 4 × 16 = 64         │ ~164  │
  │ weeks)                  │ 100             │                     │       │
  ├─────────────────────────┼─────────────────┼─────────────────────┼───────┤
  │ 5 drones (today)        │ 3/frame × 50 =  │ 5 × 16 = 80         │ ~230  │
  │                         │ 150             │                     │       │
  └─────────────────────────┴─────────────────┴─────────────────────┴───────┘

  The fifth drone costs a whole extra broadcast packet per mocap frame — +50
  packets/s — because of that 2-per-packet packing. So it isn't "5 drones is too
  many"; it's that 4→5 is a 40% jump, and the unicast half is the expensive
  kind (every packet wants an ACK and gets retried). Past the ceiling, one
  drone's retries steal airtime from the rest — which is exactly the symptom
  that moved from cf1 to cf2 when you moved the antenna.

  The cheapest fix is the telemetry, which the drones don't need in order to
  fly:

● Both rates are in (pose 2 Hz, status 1 Hz, kalman_preflight 2 Hz). Your stack
  is still running untouched — the yaml is read only at launch, so this applies
  on your next relaunch. Here's what it buys and what the bigger levers are:

  Per-drone unicast telemetry: 16 → 5 packets/s. For five drones that's 80 → 25
  pkt/s, taking you from ~230 pkt/s back to ~175 — below the four-drone load
  that has been fine for weeks, while still keeping five drones.

  What I did not touch, and why: the max_unicast_latency: 10 ms warning
  threshold. It's aggressive for five drones, but those warnings are what let me
  find this today — raising it would just hide the next one. I did fix the
  mocap band ([80,120] → [40,60]), which was crying wolf on every launch about
  your perfectly healthy 50 Hz stream.

  Three bigger levers, in order of effect:

  1. Halve the mocap broadcast — 150 pkt/s is the single largest consumer.
     Forwarding every Motive frame at 50 Hz is more than the onboard Kalman
     needs; decimating to 25 Hz saves 75 pkt/s. That's a small patch to
     posesChanged with a configurable rate, and it's the one I'd do next if the
     telemetry cut isn't enough.
  2. Second dongle — the real answer for five-plus drones, and this rig used to
     run two (cf1 on radio://0/80/2M, cf11 on radio://1/90/1M). Split 3+2 and
     each radio carries half the traffic. The rule is channels ≥2 apart at 2M,
     or crazyflie-link-cpp refuses the pair.
  3. Position-only broadcasts pack 4 per packet instead of 2 — but that drops
     orientation, and this rig force-fuses quaternions (locSrv.extQuatStdDev = 
     0.05); losing yaw correction is the fly-away case, so I would not do this
     one.

  Plus the interference hygiene from before, which is free: Wi-Fi is still
  associated at 2427 MHz and Bluetooth is still enabled, both inside the laptop
  next to the dongle.

  One correction to my earlier message: I told you cf1's link was physically bad
  and suggested swapping the airframe. The fault moving to cf2 when you moved
  the antenna disproves that — it's contention plus RF geometry, not a bad
  drone. The "swap cf1" advice was wrong; disregard it.

  Relaunch when you're ready and I'll measure the receive rates and pose
  frequencies again — same checks, so we'll have a clean before/after.

  You're right that you may have been lucky, and the data supports your 
  skepticism over my theory. The link warnings are chronic: every server log
  since June shows them, and the 09-17 show that flew cleanly had cf3 reporting 
  a low receive rate in 98 of its 435 seconds (22%), with momentary 0.00
  dropouts in every run including yesterday's successful arena sweeps. So this
  rig's telemetry has always been lossy and it flies anyway. My "4→5 drones
  overloaded the radio" story was wrong, and "the console clogs the radio" is
  unproven.

  The one defect I'm confident about is mine, and it plausibly explains
  console-vs-manual without any radio difference: the placement gate gave each
  drone 6 seconds to produce one /cfX/pose, on a rig that routinely goes silent
  for 10+ seconds. Anything that slows the machine down makes that 6 s window
  likelier to be missed — so the same rig passes manually and fails under load,
  with no RF change at all. It's now 25 s and reports which drones it's still
  waiting for.

  What I did find in the console (code, not measurement — so a hypothesis, not a
  verdict):

  - _check_drones runs on every sweep, only while the server is up, and spawns
    ros2 topic echo --once twice per drone — ~14 fresh ros2 CLI processes per
    sweep, each a full rclpy node and DDS participant.
  - Each of those probes blocks up to 6 s when a drone's telemetry is lossy.
  - There's no lock around the sweep, so if a sweep outlasts the poll interval,
    sweeps overlap.

  Together that's self-amplifying: lossy link → probes block → overlapping
  sweeps → more concurrent processes → more CPU contention → more missed
  telemetry. It's CPU and DDS churn rather than RF, but the effect on the gate
  is the same. That's worth fixing regardless of today's cause — a lock plus
  skipping per-drone probes while a flight script is running.

  To settle it properly rather than by luck, bring the stack up and I'll sample
  the server's warning rate and process churn for 60 s with the console open,
  then 60 s with it closed. Same measurement both ways — that's a real answer in
  about three minutes, no flying required.

  One thing I need to know: did the show actually take off and land? If it flew,
  that's the constellation show's first hardware flight and changes what I'd
  record (and I'd want the flown separation numbers from /tf).

  - 2–3 drones deliver telemetry; the rest are starved, and which ones rotates

  - wrong latency id shows replies arriving a ping or more late, out of order —

  rather you pick than have me change the rig again mid-session:

  1. Fly anyway. Flight doesn't need telemetry: position updates to the drones
     are broadcasts, which carry no ACK and aren't retried. The only thing
     stopping you is my placement gate wanting one /cfX/pose per drone within 25
     s. If you want, I can add an explicit opt-in (allow_mocap_only:=true) that
     accepts a drone proven tracked by /poses alone, with a loud warning — it
     weakens the end-to-end check, so it's your call, not mine.
  2. Cut telemetry to 2 Hz — the one lever that directly reduces unicast
     airtime. I reverted it because the 10 Hz config is upstream's and worked
     for weeks, so it isn't the cause; but it is a legitimate mitigation for a
     backlogged link. Testable in one relaunch.
  3. Second dongle — the structural fix for five drones. This rig ran two
     historically (channels ≥2 apart at 2M). Halves the airtime per radio and
     removes the retry coupling between drones entirely.
  4. Power-cycle cf3 and cf5 and check their antennas aren't pinned under a
     battery strap or marker deck. Cheapest thing to try first, and it costs you
     one relaunch.

  My honest read: given it rotates between airframes and predates today, (3) is
  the real answer and (4) is worth 60 seconds before anything else. Which do you
  want?

    shell, the repo, or any rc file. Good suspect, not guilty.

  single stick that's identical, but if you ever plug both in, radio://0 is "the
  first one enumerated" — which may be either stick. If you want, I'll switch
  the whole fleet to address the dongle by serial instead, which is unambiguous.

● The console runs the stack through a pty and pumps every line to the browser.

  Searched for 1 pattern (ctrl+o to expand)

● The pump and bus look non-blocking, so the stall must be further down. There's
  one mechanism that fits your observation exactly and is directly observable:
  the console gives the stack a pty, whose kernel buffer is ~4 KB, and
  crazyflie_server emits hundreds of warning lines per second when links are
  marginal. If the reader can't keep up, the server's write() blocks in the tty 
  layer — stalling the thread that services the radio, which produces more
  warnings. A terminal drains far faster, so manual runs never hit it.

  That's testable: a blocked thread shows up in /proc. Let me check the current
  state:

  What survives, and now has supporting evidence: the console's periodic 
  probing, which happens only while a browser page is open. Each live sweep
  spawns ~14 ros2 CLI processes — 10 of them ros2 topic echo /cfX/{pose,status} 
  --once with a 6 s timeout each, so they linger exactly when a drone is slow.
  That's a fresh DDS participant joining and leaving the domain every few
  seconds, continuously, which the server's single-threaded executor has to
  service. And the correlation holds in both directions: your bad runs were with
  the dashboard open; your good manual runs — and the state I just sampled —
  had no page connected and zero probe processes.

  The test that settles it (two minutes, no flying, same dongle and stack):

  1. Launch the stack manually, page closed. I measure pose rates and warning
     counts for 15 s.
  2. You open the console dashboard. Wait ~60 s for several sweeps.
  3. I measure again. Rates drop and wrong latency id appears → confirmed.
  4. You close the page. I measure once more to see it recover.

  Only the browser page changes between the three samples, which is as clean as
  this gets.

  If it confirms, the fix is small and I'd propose: drop the per-drone topic 
  echo --once probes from the live sweep (keep them in the on-demand full sweep,
  or run them only when you open that drone's panel), and add a "flight mode"
  that pauses probing entirely while a flight script is running. The console's
  design rule — CLI, never in-process rclpy — stays intact; it just stops
  probing five drones every few seconds while you're trying to fly.

  Say when you've launched and I'll take sample 1.

● Here's the consolidated picture. I'll be direct about what's proven and what
  isn't.

  The console is not the cause — my own test says so. Sample 1 (page closed) and
  sample 2 (dashboard open, 12 connections, probing continuously) were both
  clean, all five at 10 Hz. Sample 3 (console-launched) failed with cf2/cf5 down

  causation. Everything I proposed today — packet rates, scanning, CPU load,
  pty back-pressure, apt shadowing, DDS config, lost log-block setup — is
  eliminated, several by my own measurements.

  What the failure actually is, precisely: the affected drones stay connected
  and their latency pings do reply, just late (hence wrong latency id, which
  tracks the silent drones exactly: cf2=5, cf5=6, cf1/cf3=0). Their ROS
  publishers exist. The server simply receives no log data from them. So their
  downlink carries occasional small packets but can't sustain 16 log packets/s —
  an RF margin problem, re-rolled each launch, which is why the victim set
  changes and a relaunch sometimes fixes it.

  One real interference source, measured: four APs on Wi-Fi channel 11 (2462
  MHz) at strong signal, plus your own laptop Wi-Fi transmitting at 2427 MHz
  inches from the dongle. Channel 80 (2480 MHz) sits just above their span, so
  it's adjacent-channel leakage rather than a direct hit — enough to eat margin
  on a weak link, not enough to explain everything.

  To fly tonight: relaunch and check all five publish before committing — ros2 
  topic hz /cfX/pose, or the console's health page, takes seconds. The show's
  gates now catch the bad case up front (25 s pose window, supervisor check)
  instead of refusing after a 55 s upload. Right now you'd be flying four.

  Two things I'd offer, your pick:

  1. A telemetry watchdog in the server — warn loudly when a connected drone
     delivers no log data for N seconds. That converts this silent failure into
     a named fault the preflight GUI and console can show immediately, and it
     would capture the next occurrence with data instead of us reconstructing it
     from logs. Small patch to crazyflie_server.cpp.
  2. Margin, in order of effect: dongle on a USB extension with line of sight
     (biggest single lever on downlink), laptop Wi-Fi off during flights
     (ethernet already serves Motive), then the second dongle.

  Which do you want — the watchdog, or shall I leave the rig alone so you can
  re-roll the launch and fly?

● I think I have it. Look at what the log-block creation accepts as success:

  if (result != crtpLogControlResultOk && result !=
  crtpLogControlResultBlockExists)
      throw ...

  BlockExists is treated as OK. Log block IDs are assigned per drone starting at
  0, so if a drone still holds blocks from a previous server session, the new
  server's create returns "exists", it's accepted, and start() then starts the
  old block — whose variable layout may not match what the new server expects.
  Connected, acked, no usable telemetry, no error. And blocks only survive if
  the previous server died before its destructors ran:

● Bash(cd ~/.ros/log && echo "=== did crazyflie_server shut down cleanly, or get

     void Crazyflie::logReset()

● logReset(), block creation, and start() are all acknowledged — so the blocks
  really are running on the drone with the right variables. That eliminates the
  leftover-block race too, and it leaves one mechanism standing, which I should
  have reasoned about much earlier:

  A Crazyflie can only send data when the dongle polls it. Log data rides in the
  ACK payload of a poll — it is not something the drone can push. So a drone's
  telemetry rate is set by how often the link layer gets round to its address.
  When one connection goes into a retry storm, the others get polled less often,
  their firmware log queues fill, and the firmware drops packets. The starved
  drone still answers the occasional latency ping (hence wrong latency id — late
  replies), but its 16 packets/s of telemetry never escape. Which drones starve
  depends on who starts storming, which is why the victims change every launch
  while nothing in the configuration does.

  That also explains every observation that defeated my earlier theories: it's
  not the console, not the argv, not the APs, not firmware, not the dongle
  serial — those were all constant, and this mechanism is dynamic.

  So the levers are about airtime, and I want to be careful here: I reverted the
  telemetry cut earlier because the 10 Hz config is upstream's and worked for
  weeks — that reasoning still holds, it is not the cause. But it is now the
  cheapest mitigation with an actual mechanism behind it: pose 10→2 Hz and
  kalman_preflight 5→2 Hz takes each drone from 16 to 5 packets/s, which is a 3×
  reduction in queue pressure and polling demand. Your preflight GUI and the
  show's gates both work fine at 2 Hz.

  My recommendation, in order:

  1. Cut the telemetry rates (one yaml edit, one relaunch) — tests the mechanism
     immediately and may unblock you tonight.
  2. Add a per-drone telemetry watchdog to the server: warn when a connected
     drone delivers no log data for N seconds. Right now this failure is
     completely silent — the server prints "Logging to /pose at 10 Hz" and then
     nothing, forever. That line is printed on intent, before setup even
     happens, which is why nothing in the log told us.
  3. Second dongle when you're ready — it splits the polling between two radios
     and removes the coupling between drones entirely. You said you'd rather
     understand first; now there's a mechanism to justify it.

  Want me to do 1 and 2 now? Both are small and reversible, and 2 means the next
  occurrence reports itself instead of us reconstructing it from six hours of
  logs.

  ┌───────────────────────────────┬───────────┬──────────────────┬──────────┐
  │             case              │ handshake │  per-drone rate  │ warnings │
  ├───────────────────────────────┼───────────┼──────────────────┼──────────┤

  │                               │           │  20 s            │          │
  ├───────────────────────────────┼───────────┼──────────────────┼──────────┤
  │ console-like (pty + setsid +  │ 20 s      │ ~7.1 Hz, one 3 s │ 0        │
  │ nofile + env)                 │           │  gap             │          │
  ├───────────────────────────────┼───────────┼──────────────────┼──────────┤
  │ same console-like stack, 3    │ —         │ 10.0 Hz all five │ 0        │
  │ min later                     │           │                  │          │
  └───────────────────────────────┴───────────┴──────────────────┴──────────┘

  The within-session drift is larger than any difference between the cases, so a
  60-second sample can't distinguish them — and neither can one run each, which
  is all my earlier samples were. To resolve a difference this noisy I'd need
  ~10 minutes per case, repeated and interleaved, which is 40+ minutes of your
  lab time.

  There's a better way that costs you nothing: instrument it passively. A small
  recorder that samples every 30 s — per-drone pose rate, wrong latency id and
  low-rx counts, mocap rate — and appends to a CSV, tagging each row with how
  the stack was launched. It can detect that itself: the parent of the ros2 
  launch process is either mission_console or a shell. Then you fly normally,
  launching however you like, and after a few sessions the data says whether
  console-launched stacks are statistically worse, with effect size rather than
  anecdote. If they are, the CSV also tells us when within a session it
  diverges, which is the clue the mechanism hunt has been missing.

  Want me to write that? It's read-only, runs in the background, and touches
  nothing in the flight path.

  Meanwhile the rig is healthy right now — all five at 10.0 Hz, handshake 20 s —
  so if you'd rather fly the show while it's cooperating, that's the better use
  of the window. The stack currently up is the one my harness launched (same
  arguments as the console's); say the word and I'll hand it back by relaunching
  however you prefer.

  server logs zero "unrequested data for block" warnings, which is what you'd
  see if packets arrived and were discarded. The drone simply stops sending log
  data while remaining fully responsive to anything the server initiates.

  2. Power cycling isn't needed — reboot() exists over the radio. crazyflie_cpp
  exposes reboot(), rebootFromBootloader() and sysoff(), so a wedged drone can
  be restarted from the keyboard with no hands on batteries. And if the real
  mechanism turns out to be load-related, recovery may need nothing more than
  restarting that drone's log blocks.

  3. Why don't they stop together? Because whatever stalls is per-drone state,
  not a shared resource. A dongle or server fault would take all five at once; a
  radio-wide problem would hit the weakest first and recover together. Instead
  two specific drones are dead while three are perfect on the same dongle, same
  server, same instant — and cf5's and cf4's links are indistinguishable from 
  the healthy three by every link-level metric available.

  I also owe you a correction on the thing I said two messages ago: I claimed
  the 1 Hz status block surviving would disprove starvation. That reasoning was
  wrong — the server-initiated ping and the drone's queued log data compete for
  the same return slot, so a ping can survive while log data starves. So I can't
  yet separate "drone-side log stall" from "this drone's return slots are being
  consumed", and I'm not going to pick between them by argument after today's
  record.

  The experiment that separates them, with no C++ changes: relaunch with only
  three drones enabled. If the two previously-silent drones stay alive for ten
  minutes at three-drone load, it's slot/airtime allocation and the fix is load
  (rates, drone count, second dongle). If a drone still goes silent at
  three-drone load with a healthy link, it's a firmware log stall and the fix is
  detect-and-restart-blocks. One relaunch, ten minutes, and it decides which of
  the two remaining explanations is real.

● Now I understand what you're asking for, and there's a catch worth knowing
  before we try it.

  A logReset through the running server isn't exposed — cf5's services are only
  arm, emergency, go_to, land, notify_setpoints_stop, start_trajectory, takeoff,
  upload_trajectory. No logging control at all. So any logReset has to come
  over the radio from outside, which needs a second dongle (your 5.04 stick).

  But here's the catch: logReset clears all log blocks on that drone, including 
  the server's — and the server only creates blocks during its connect sequence,
  with no reconnect or re-setup path (I just checked: there's no
  disconnect/reconnect handling in crazyflie_server.cpp). So an external
  logReset would leave cf5 with no blocks at all, and /cf5/pose would stay
  silent regardless.

  Let me confirm what the external tool actually does, since that determines
  whether the experiment is still worth running:

● That's doable, and the tool's behaviour makes it a genuinely good experiment.
  log.cpp does requestLogToc(), then creates a block and calls start() — and
  block creation treats BlockExists as success. So aiming it at cf5 from a
  second dongle gives three distinguishable outcomes:

  1. /cf5/pose resumes — my block id collides with the server's, so start()
     kicks the server's stalled block back to life. That proves the stall is a
     stopped block and that a watchdog calling stop/start would recover it live,
     with no restart and no batteries.
  2. My tool gets data but /cf5/pose stays silent — the drone's log subsystem is
     fine and only the server's specific block is dead, so recovery needs
     delete-and-recreate rather than a kick.
  3. My tool gets nothing — the whole log subsystem on that drone is wedged, and
     a radio reboot() is the only software escape.

  All three tell us exactly what the fix must do, and none of them touch the
  server.

● Decisive — and it flips the diagnosis. The external tool streamed cf5's data
  happily at 10 Hz (kalman.stateX ≈ -0.154, which matches cf5's mark at x =
  −0.155), while /cf5/pose stayed silent the whole time.

  So cf5's logging subsystem is fine. Its link is fine. It will log for anyone
  who asks afresh. Also note the tool's timestamps: 7863598 ms ≈ 2.2 hours of 
  uptime, so cf5 never rebooted. The fault is in the server's own log blocks —
  they've been stopped on the drone, and nothing recreates them.

  That points at a specific, very fixable class of bug. Let me check how the
  server owns those block objects:

  Searched for 1 pattern (ctrl+o to expand)

● Your auto-logReset idea wouldn't fix this, and the experiment just proved why.
  cf5's logging subsystem is healthy — it created a block and streamed at 10 Hz
  for the second dongle, with 2.2 hours of uptime and no reboot. So there's
  nothing on the drone to reset.

  Combined with the rest of today's evidence, the fault is now localised to the 
  server's link layer not collecting downlink from that connection:

  - The drone logs fine for a fresh connection (just demonstrated).
  - The server's unique_ptr/list block storage can't be accidentally destroyed,
    so the server didn't stop them.
  - Outgoing packets to cf5 work — the LED param write succeeded while it was
    silent, and it did not restore telemetry.
  - The server still pings cf5 ~0.84 times/s, and those pings are the only
    traffic it schedules for that connection.

  In this protocol a drone can only send inside the reply to a poll. cf5 gets ~1
  poll/s, and the ping reply consumes that one reply slot — so log data never
  gets a turn, which is exactly why all three blocks read 0.00 Hz rather than
  "degraded". The drone isn't stalling; the dongle stopped polling it for data.

  That's a bug in crazyflie-link-cpp's per-connection scheduling, not in the
  drone, not in the config, and not in the console. It also explains every
  oddity: random victims, frozen for the session, links that look perfect, and a
  relaunch fixing it (a new connection gets scheduled afresh).

  So the recovery you want — fix it without restarting the server — is a 
  per-drone reconnect, not a logReset: tear down and rebuild that one drone's
  connection in place, which re-runs logReset, recreates the blocks and gets it
  scheduled again. That's implementable in crazyflie_server.cpp as exactly the
  watchdog you described, with the right action attached: link alive but no log 
  data for N seconds → rebuild that connection.

  Two honest caveats before you decide: I can't yet say why the scheduler drops
  a connection (that's inside the link library, and I'd want to read its radio
  loop properly rather than guess again), and a per-drone reconnect takes a few
  seconds during which that drone has no telemetry — fine on the ground, not
  something you'd want firing mid-flight, so it should be disabled while armed.

  Want me to read the link library's radio loop first to find the actual
  trigger, or go straight to implementing the watchdog with per-drone reconnect?

✻ Baked for 50s · done Friday 5:44 PM

› Message from general-purpose
  Verdict: the hypothesis (loop stops polling one connection) is largely REFUTED
  by the code. The loop polls every connection every iteration. The code does
  support a different mechanism, below.

  Paths are relative to crazyflie-link-cpp/src/.

  Q1. Servicing and empty polls (CrazyradioThread.cpp:161-375)
  - There is no scheduler. Each iteration copies the whole connection set (:170)
  and loops over every connection (:192).
  - Empty polls are sent. The only skip is:
  ```
  195: if (con->queue_send_.empty() && !con->retry_ && !con->useAutoPing_)
  continue;
  ```
  - For a normal URI, useAutoPing_ is true (Connection.cpp:80), so nothing is
  skipped. Safelink path: `Packet p(ping, sizeof(ping))` is the default, and at
  :273-279 an empty queue gives a ping, `++sent_ping_count`. Inline mode just
  swaps the transmit call for `sendPacketInline(...)`.

  Q2. Skip, backoff or removal conditions
  - Broadcast priority (:182-202): non-broadcast connections are skipped only
  while a broadcast connection has queued packets. A normal server does not hold
  such a connection open persistently, so this is unlikely.
  - No backoff, error counter, or removal on ack failure. On a failed ack,
  `con->retry_ = p` (:297) and the same packet is resent next iteration. The
  packet stays in `retry_` until it is acked.
  - Safelink discard (:301-306):
  ```
  if (ack && ack.size()>0 && (ack.data()[0]&0x04)==(con->safelinkDown_<<2))
  safelinkDown_=!safelinkDown_;
  else ack = Crazyradio::Ack();   // reset ack to invalid since safelink does
  not match
  ```
  - If safelinkDown_ ever disagrees with the drone's down bit, every
  non-matching downlink ack is silently dropped.
  - Nothing resets that mismatch except toggling to match.
  - safelinkUp_ toggles on any ack (:292), so uplink looks fine.
  - Thread-wide: any libusb error throws runtime_error and kills the thread
  (:99-116). That sets runtime_error_ on all connections, so it would hit all
  drones, not one.

  Q3. Inline mode
  - The host loop still issues every poll. setInlineMode
  (Crazyradio.cpp:116-118) is only a vendor request, 0x23.
  - Each poll is a USB bulk write carrying a per-packet header of length,
  datarate|ack, channel and the 5 address bytes (Crazyradio.cpp:254-263). The
  dongle holds no polling set and runs no autonomous polling.
  - A connection cannot drop out of a dongle-side set, because none exists.

  Q4. Bounded receive queue
  - None. queue_recv_ is an unbounded `std::priority_queue`
  (ConnectionImpl.h:44), pushed at CrazyradioThread.cpp:369 with no size check.
  It is popped in Connection.cpp:285. A full queue cannot explain the symptom.
  - The one filter drops empty acks: `useAckFilter_ && ack.size()==0` (:357).

  Q5. Ping versus log path
  - Same radio path for both. Pings are auto-ping packets, or a queued 0xFF/echo
  packet. Log data comes back in the ACK to any poll and is pushed to
  queue_recv_. The latency echo is also an ack payload.
  - Difference at the server level: if the echo returns but log packets never
  arrive, the break is above the link, in port/channel dispatch. Check
  crazyflie_server.cpp and Crazyflie.cpp.
  - Alternatively, the safelink discard would drop everything including echoes,
  which is why pings surviving argues against a safelink mismatch.

  Secondary suspect: inline-mode ack read desync
  - sendPacketInline writes, then reads the reply with a 10 ms timeout. On
  LIBUSB_ERROR_TIMEOUT it returns an empty Ack (Crazyradio.cpp:285-296).
  - If the dongle's reply arrives later, it is the next read's result. Replies
  are then off by one and can be attributed to the wrong connection.
  - Effects: safelink bits flip incorrectly, and acks or log data are dropped or
  mis-assigned.
  - This is plausible with 5 drones on one radio. It would be random per
  session, and it would be stable once a connection's safelink phase is out of
  sync.
  - This is inference from the code, not proven.

  Suggested test: log `statistics_` per connection (sent_ping_count, ack_count,
  receive_count). If ack_count rises while receive_count is flat, the safelink
  discard or ack filter is the cause. If sent_count is flat, the loop is not
  polling that connection.

● The Sonnet agent refuted my polling hypothesis with code: there is no
  scheduler, every connection is serviced every iteration, and useAutoPing_ is
  true for normal URIs so nothing is ever skipped (CrazyradioThread.cpp:195).
  Empty polls are always sent. Good — that's one more of my theories dead, and
  this time on evidence rather than vibes.

  It surfaced two mechanisms the code does support:

  1. Safelink discard (CrazyradioThread.cpp:301-306): if safelinkDown_ ever
     disagrees with the drone's down bit, every mismatching downlink ack is
     silently dropped and nothing resets it. But that would kill the latency
     echoes too — and your pings survive — so this argues against it.
  2. Inline-mode ack read desync — and this one is interesting, because inline 
     mode is Crazyradio 2.0 only. sendPacketInline writes, then reads the reply
     with a 10 ms timeout; on timeout it returns an empty ack
     (Crazyradio.cpp:285-296). If the dongle's reply lands later, it becomes the
     next read's result — so replies go off by one and can be attributed to the
     wrong connection. That would be random per session, stable once a
     connection is out of phase, and more likely with five drones sharing one
     radio. Exactly the shape of what you've been seeing all day.

  It's inference from code, not proof. But it's testable two ways, and one is
  decisive:

  The decisive test — disable inline mode. CrazyradioThread.cpp has a single
  line, const bool supports_inline_mode = is_supported_crazyradio2;. Forcing it
  to false makes your Crazyradio 2.0 use the same transmit path a Crazyradio PA
  uses — the long-proven one. One line, one rebuild of the crazyflie package,
  trivially revertible. If the stalls stop, we've found it and the fix is
  upstream-reportable. If they continue, inline mode is cleared and we move to
  the next candidate with no ambiguity.

  The cheaper evidence — per-connection counters. server.yaml has publish_stats:
  false; turning it on publishes sent/ack/receive counts per drone. Then at the
  moment of a stall: if ack_count rises while receive_count stays flat, it's
  the discard/filter path; if sent_count is flat, it's polling after all. This
  needs a server restart, so it costs nothing if folded into your next launch.

  My recommendation: do both on the next relaunch — flip publish_stats on, and
  try the one-line inline-mode change. That's a ~2 minute setup and it either
  names the bug or eliminates the leading suspect.

  Want me to make those two changes? The inline-mode one touches vendored
  third-party code, so I'll hold until you say go.

› Message from general-purpose
  **Bottom line.** Nothing found upstream describes this exact symptom. Two
  leads: (1) the dongle's 5.01 may really be firmware 5.1, which Bitcraze
  retired; (2) the vendored link library has a likely-benign gap at the 10 ms
  read timeout. The inline-mode desync is still unproven and should be tested,
  not assumed.

  **1. Vendored version (verified unless marked)**
  - No git metadata, VENDORED.md or submodule pin exists under
  `src/crazyswarm2/crazyflie/deps/`. Only `crazyflie-link-cpp/version` = "1.0a4"
  and `.gitmodules` pointing at bitcraze/crazyflie-link-cpp and
  whoenig/crazyflie_cpp.
  - The exact commit is not recoverable. The tree was imported in commit
  80fc0a4, dated 2026-06-24.
  - The vendored copy contains inline mode (`Crazyradio.cpp:116, 240-303`;
  `CrazyradioThread.cpp:150-158`). It also has broadcast prioritisation
  (`CrazyradioThread.cpp:182-201`, upstream PR #30, merged 2024-01-30).
  - Upstream bitcraze master has no inline-mode PR. I checked
  https://github.com/bitcraze/crazyflie-link-cpp/pulls?q=is%3Apr, which lists
  none, and the commit list ends at "Include string library for stricter
  compilers" (2025-12-10). Newer merged PRs are #40 (visibility settings,
  2026-05-27) and the stricter-compiler include fix (#38). So the inline code
  comes from a Crazyswarm2-side fork or patch.
  - Upstream commits since the vendored state are therefore build or visibility
  changes only. None touch inline mode, ack handling, safelink or Crazyradio 2.0
  support. I could not verify the fork's history: the
  whoenig/crazyflie-link-cpp and IMRCLab/crazyflie_tools commit pages returned
  404.
  - The only Crazyradio 2.0 commit in upstream is PR #35, "Add proper broadcast
  version check to support Crazyradio 2.0 broadcasting" (2023-08-02). Vendored
  line 132 already carries it.

  **2. Known reports**
  - No match for this symptom class in the open issues of
  bitcraze/crazyflie-link-cpp (#37 sleep vs yield, #36 broadcast and console
  example not consistent, #24 commander packets, #6 benchmark, and others).
  Nothing matches in IMRCLab/crazyswarm2 issue search for "inline mode" either.
  - Related and relevant: Bitcraze blog https://www.bitcraze.io/2025/12/new-craz
  yradio-2-0-swarm-optimized-firmware-5-1/. Pre-5.x firmware had a bug with
  high-rate broadcast mixed with unicast, seen at 8 or more drones. Inline mode
  sidesteps it, and about 1000-1200 packets/s are shared between all drones.
  - Closed-forum search found nothing about a silent per-drone log stall.

  **3. Official guidance and firmware (verified, from
  https://github.com/bitcraze/crazyradio2-firmware/releases)**
  - Release list: 5.0, 5.1, 5.2, 5.3, 5.4, 5.5.
  - 5.1 is marked "retired, >=5.2 should be used instead".
  - 5.2 fixes "malformed packet over USB when sending packet to an unsupported
  channel" in inline mode.
  - 5.3 adds RSSI to inline mode, 5.4 adds a sniffer mode, and 5.5 (latest I
  could see) adds radio test controls.
  - Inferred: bcdDevice 5.01 is probably firmware 5.1, the retired release, and
  5.04 is 5.4. Check with `lsusb -v`.
  - I found no official per-dongle drone cap. Bitcraze says 5.1 roughly doubles
  capacity, and a 49-drone cflib2 demo used a single Crazyradio 2.0. Five drones
  is far below any stated limit.
  - Inline protocol doc: https://www.bitcraze.io/documentation/repository/crazyr
  adio2-firmware/main/development/compatible-usb-protocol/. It does not say what
  the dongle replies when ack is disabled.

  **4. Ranked fixes**
  1. **Update the dongle to 5.5 (or at least 5.4).** Very low risk and
  reversible.
     - Evidence: 5.01 is likely the retired 5.1, and 5.2 fixed an inline-mode
  USB reply bug.
     - Action: flash with the Bitcraze tooling, then check `lsusb -v | grep
  bcdDevice`.
     - Success: run 30 or more minutes on the 5.01 dongle and on a 5.04 or 5.5
  dongle. See whether /cfX/pose still hits 0 Hz, and check how often it happens
  per hour. This is also a natural A/B test.
  2. **Server-side watchdog.** Medium effort, directly mitigating.
     - Evidence: the stall is per drone, frozen per session, and pings and param
  writes still work. That points to log-packet delivery or state, not a dead
  drone.
     - Action: in `crazyflie_server.cpp`, if a drone's log topics are silent for
  more than 3 s but pings succeed, disconnect and reconnect that `Crazyflie`
  object. This also retries log-block creation.
     - Success: pose resumes within seconds with no server restart.
  3. **Disable inline mode as a diagnostic.** Low risk, but it may bring back
  the old broadcast bug.
     - Evidence: indirect. The late-reply desync at `Crazyradio.cpp:285-296` is
  inferred, not observed.
     - Change: `CrazyradioThread.cpp:150`, `const bool supports_inline_mode =
  false;`. A cleaner version reads `getenv("CRAZYRADIO_NO_INLINE")`.
     - Caveat: the non-inline path reconfigures the radio per connection, and
  Bitcraze says inline avoids a firmware bug with broadcast plus unicast. Fly
  only in a test.
     - Success: a long soak with no stalls. Note that cmdFullState uses
  broadcast.
  4. **Resync in the safelink and ack path.** Medium risk; needs a debug counter
  first.
     - Evidence: `CrazyradioThread.cpp:301-306` discards mismatching acks
  forever, which matches "pings succeed but the data stream is dead".
     - Sketch: count consecutive mismatches per connection. After about 50, set
  `con->safelinkDown_ = !con->safelinkDown_`, or re-send `enableSafelink` (line
  251).
     - Instrument first: log the counter when pose is at 0 Hz. If it is
  climbing, this is the cause.
  5. **Harden the inline read at `Crazyradio.cpp:285-296`.** Medium risk, needs
  bench verification.
     - Evidence: speculative, it depends on late replies arriving after the
  timeout.
     - Sketch: after a timeout, drain the IN endpoint with a short read loop
  before the next write. Optionally raise the 10 ms timeout.
  6. **Update the link library.** Low value. Upstream has no relevant commits
  (see section 1). Rebasing onto a newer Crazyswarm2 fork may carry inline
  fixes, but I could not verify that.

  **Evidence against the inline suspicion.** Pings round-trip at 10-35 ms, and
  param writes are acknowledged. If replies were desynced, ping acks would be
  misattributed too. That is not proof, since packet-type routing could still
  survive a desync. Verify with fix 4's counter and fix 3's A/B test.

› Message from general-purpose
  **Latest release: 5.5** (github.com/bitcraze/crazyradio2-firmware/releases).
  Everything below was fetched, not run on hardware. Release dates conflicted
  between two fetches: one gave 2025/2026, the other 2023/2024. Treat the dates
  as unverified. The version order is solid.

  **1. Asset**
  - Download `crazyradio2-5.5.uf2`. The pattern is `crazyradio2-<ver>.uf2`.
  - The format is UF2, a drag-and-drop file for the bootloader's mass-storage
  drive. It is not DFU and not a zip.
  - Do not use the old `crazyradio2-CRPA-emulation-*.uf2/.hex` files (1.x
  firmware).

  **2. Bootloader mode**
  - Hold the button while plugging the dongle into USB. The LED pulses slowly
  red.
  - It then mounts as a mass-storage drive named `Crazyradio2`.
  - Sources: bitcraze.io/documentation/repository/crazyradio2-firmware/main/buil
  ding-and-flashing/flash/ and the Crazyradio 2.0 product page.

  **3. Flash commands (Ubuntu 22.04)**
  No tool install is needed. Desktop Ubuntu normally auto-mounts the drive under
  `/media/$USER/Crazyradio2`.
  ```
  # 1. Stop the ROS stack and anything else holding the dongle.
  # 2. Unplug, hold the button, plug in, release once the LED is pulsing red.
  lsblk -o NAME,LABEL | grep -i crazyradio2     # confirm the volume appeared
  cp ~/Downloads/crazyradio2-5.5.uf2 /media/$USER/Crazyradio2/
  sync
  ```
  - If it does not auto-mount, run `udisksctl mount -b /dev/sdX1`, taking the
  device name from `lsblk`.
  - The dongle reboots by itself after a few seconds. Do one dongle at a time so
  you know which is which.
  - The `adafruit-nrfutil` serial method in the docs is for firmware developers
  building from source, not for this job. I did not test it.

  **4. Verify**
  ```
  lsusb -d 1915:7777 -v 2>/dev/null | grep bcdDevice
  ```
  - I expect `5.05` for firmware 5.5.
  - That is an inference from your 5.01 and 5.04 readings, which map to 5.1 and
  5.4. The docs do not state it. Firmware 5.0 changed the reported device
  version from the Zephyr version to the firmware version, so this mapping holds
  for 5.x.

  **5. Warnings**
  - **Bricking:** I found no brick warning, and the bootloader is built in. The
  docs say upgrades need no extra hardware. Your dongle should stay recoverable
  by redoing the button-hold. I could not find explicit wording on bootloader
  recovery.
  - **Erase:** I found nothing saying settings are erased. I did not verify
  this.
  - **ROS stack:** I found no doc statement on this. Stop the ROS stack first
  anyway, because a replug during flashing drops the device. The server holds it
  open (`ros2 launch` / crazyflie_server).
  - **Host libraries:** 5.0 and later report the firmware version, and 5.1 added
  "inline mode". This lets one dongle serve many Crazyflies at about 1000
  packets/s total. No release note I saw sets a minimum cflib or
  crazyflie-link-cpp version. Bitcraze lists current cflib as 0.1.34 and
  CFclient as 2026.8.1. For the workspace's crazyflie-link-cpp, check the
  Crazyswarm2 / crazyflie-link-cpp release notes for 5.x support. I could not
  verify that. After flashing, test with `scan` before a flight.

  **6. Release notes, 5.1 to 5.5**
  - **5.1 (retired):** introduced inline mode, roughly 1000 packets/s across all
  drones, and made the Crazyradio 2.0 officially supported by Crazyswarm. The
  release says to use 5.2 or later.
  - **5.2:** fixes malformed USB responses in inline mode when a packet is sent
  to an unsupported channel.
  - **5.3:** adds RSSI to inline mode.
  - **5.4:** adds a sniffer mode, which observes packets and lets the PC
  broadcast for P2P networks.
  - **5.5:** adds USB control for low-level radio tests.

  Both dongles should go to 5.5. The 5.1 one is the priority, because its
  inline-mode bug is fixed in 5.2 and later.

● Good news on the audit, with one real caveat. Here's what I changed around the
  yaml work and what it can touch:

  Cannot affect the radio (file paths and launch plumbing only): deleting the
  duplicate crazyflie_shows/config/*.yaml, pointing plan_show/show_launch.py at
  the workspace yaml, the CMake install change, the server:= launch argument,
  the mocap warning band, and publish_stats (which I only enabled after the
  stalls started).

  Two changes that do touch it:

  1. cf1's URI, radio://*/ → radio://0/. I assumed this was a no-op on a
     single-dongle rig. It isn't provably so: Connection.cpp:113-117 maps * to
     devid_ = -1, and that value takes a different path in USBManager — a fixed
     id is pinned (USBManager.cpp:382), while -1 goes through an assignment path
     that can also re-home a connection between radios (:323-324), and
     Connection::close() skips removeConnection entirely for a wildcard (:147).
     So cf1 — the drone that was on the wildcard for weeks, and the first one to
     stall today — now takes a different code path than it did. I can't claim
     that caused this, but I can't claim it's harmless either, and it's a
     one-line revert.
  2. The fleet composition: cf8 out, cf4 in. The configuration that "was fine
     for weeks" had cf8 as the fifth drone, not cf4. cf4 hadn't flown in weeks.
     Its firmware matches the others, but it's a different airframe in the
     shared-radio mix.

  On the firmware front, the research agent found something concrete: your 5.01
  dongle is firmware 5.1, which Bitcraze marks "retired — use ≥5.2", and 5.2 
  specifically fixes malformed USB responses in inline mode. Releases run to 5.5
  (5.3 adds RSSI, 5.4 a sniffer mode). Updating is drag-and-drop: hold the
  dongle's button while plugging in (LED pulses red), it mounts as a Crazyradio2
  volume, copy crazyradio2-5.5.uf2 onto it, sync. Verify with lsusb -d 
  1915:7777 -v | grep bcdDevice → expect 5.05. No tools to install, bootloader
  is built in, stop the stack first. Caveat I want to flag: today's stalls 
  happened on both sticks — cf1 starved around 15:30 while the 5.04 was plugged
  in, and the later ones were on 5.01 — so firmware alone doesn't explain it,
  though removing a retired version from the equation is still worth doing.

  Ranked plan for when you're back, cheapest and most diagnostic first:

  1. Start scripts/link_stall_recorder.py before anything else — then the next
     stall records its own counters and a 60 s precursor for every drone.
     publish_stats is already on, so the counters will be there.
  2. Revert cf1 to the wildcard URI (one line) and run a soak. That isolates my
     only radio-touching change against the "fine for weeks" baseline.
  3. Flash both dongles to 5.5, then soak again — a clean A/B against a
     known-retired firmware.
  4. Scan each address with drones powered off one at a time. Earlier,
     0xE7E7E7E703 and 0xE7E7E7E705 answered at both 1M and 2M, which I wrote off
     as scan flakiness — but two airframes sharing an address would produce
     exactly that, and would also explain pings succeeding while log data is
     incoherent. CLAUDE.md records that cf4's airframe was once re-addressed to
     become cf3, so it's worth ruling out physically.
  5. Only then the code candidates — the inline-mode kill switch (one line,
     supports_inline_mode = false) or the server watchdog.

  Already done while you were out: the recorder, publish_stats on, and four new
  CLAUDE.md gotchas covering the stall, the second-dongle probe trick, the
  supervisor-LOCKED latch, and the two logging traps that cost us hours —
  including my own false timeline, so the next session doesn't repeat it.

❯ If I were to flash the updated firmware, can I also revert back just in case? 

● Short answer: yes — and you have a zero-risk version of it available, because
  you own two dongles.

  Let me verify the rollback specifics rather than assume:
  ⎿  Resuming agent a57552c

● While that verifies the details, here's the practical answer you can act on
  regardless:

  You have two dongles, so you don't need rollback to be possible — you need 
  only flash one. Update the 5.01 stick (the retired 5.1) to 5.5 and leave the
  5.04 one untouched. That gives you:

  - a guaranteed fallback: if 5.5 misbehaves, unplug and use the 5.04 stick, no
    flashing involved;
  - a controlled A/B for the stall, which is what we actually want: same rig,
    same drones, two dongles differing only in firmware.

  On reversibility itself, the mechanism is inherently repeatable — the
  bootloader lives on the dongle and is entered by holding the button while
  plugging in, so flashing is "mount a drive, copy a file" as many times as you
  like, in either direction. Older releases are published on the same GitHub
  releases page, so going back should just be copying crazyradio2-5.1.uf2
  instead of crazyradio2-5.5.uf2.

  Two things I want confirmed before you rely on that, which is what the agent
  is checking: whether the older .uf2 assets are genuinely still downloadable
  (worth grabbing before you flash — download crazyradio2-5.1.uf2 and
  crazyradio2-5.4.uf2 and keep them locally), and whether anything refuses a
  downgrade or could leave a dongle unable to re-enter bootloader mode.

  One thing to keep in mind about the test itself: today's stalls happened on
  both dongles — cf1 starved at ~15:30 on the 5.04 stick, and the later failures
  were on 5.01. So I'd expect firmware to change the frequency, not to
  eliminate the problem. That's still useful (it either promotes or demotes the
  inline-mode theory), but don't be surprised if a stall survives the update —
  and start scripts/link_stall_recorder.py for the soak either way, so whatever
  happens is measured rather than remembered.

› Message from general-purpose
  Follow-up answers. Sources: the GitHub releases API for
  bitcraze/crazyradio2-firmware, the Bitcraze flashing doc (bitcraze.io/document
  ation/repository/crazyradio2-firmware/main/building-and-flashing/flash/), and
  the repo overview page. These are page-reading results, nothing tested on
  hardware.

  1. **Older .uf2 assets: yes, all are listed.** Each release has one asset:
     - `crazyradio2-5.0.uf2`
     - `crazyradio2-5.1.uf2`
     - `crazyradio2-5.2.uf2`
     - `crazyradio2-5.3.uf2`
     - `crazyradio2-5.4.uf2`
     - `crazyradio2-5.5.uf2`
     - The URL pattern is `https://github.com/bitcraze/crazyradio2-firmware/rele
  ases/download/<ver>/crazyradio2-<ver>.uf2`.
     - The 5.0 notes carry a known issue: crashes with Crazyswarm on mocap
  swarms of 8 or more drones. That is not relevant to your five-drone fleet.
     - Version 5.1 is marked "retired", but its asset is still listed. I did not
  download any file to confirm the links resolve.

  2. **Downgrade support:** the docs are silent. They describe only "drag the
  .UF2 onto the Crazyradio2 drive". I found no mention of anti-rollback, a
  version check or signing. I will not infer that downgrading works.

  3. **Bootloader vs. application:** the docs are silent on this. The only
  flashing instructions are for the `.uf2` application file, and no release is
  named as a bootloader update. All assets are `crazyradio2-<ver>.uf2`, with no
  bootloader-specific name. The common nRF52 pattern of a separate bootloader
  partition is unconfirmed for this dongle.

  4. **Bricking and recovery:** the docs are silent on failure modes. They give
  no warning about interrupted copies, partial writes or removing the drive
  before `sync`. Whether a bad write could stop it re-entering bootloader mode
  is unverified.

  5. **Safety net:**
     - Download `crazyradio2-5.1.uf2` and `crazyradio2-5.4.uf2` before flashing.
  Those are the versions the two dongles report today.
     - Whether per-dongle state exists, such as a serial number, calibration or
  settings, is undocumented. Nothing I read describes any, and I cannot say a
  flash would preserve it.
     - Before flashing, record `lsusb -d 1915:7777 -v | grep -E
  'bcdDevice|iSerial'` for each dongle to compare afterwards.

  **Practical advice:** flash one dongle first, the 5.1 one, and check that it
  enumerates correctly. For authoritative answers on questions 2 to 4, ask
  Bitcraze at discussions.bitcraze.io.

  (serial 62C8E949226411C0) and leave the 5.04 one (F6B6CCA0D55F5070) untouched.

  On reversibility itself, I'll separate what's verified from what isn't:

  - Verified: every release 5.0–5.5 is published with a predictable URL, the
    three files I fetched are intact, and the bootloader lives on the dongle and
    is entered by holding the button while plugging in — so flashing is
    repeatable by design, in either direction.
  - Not documented by Bitcraze: whether a downgrade is explicitly supported (no
    anti-rollback or signature check is documented — but none is promised
    either), whether an application .uf2 could ever touch the bootloader

    say "drag the .UF2 onto the Crazyradio2 drive" and go no further. For an
    authoritative answer that's a question for discussions.bitcraze.io.

  Practical upshot: stop the stack, hold the button while plugging in, copy the
  file, sync, don't interrupt it, verify bcdDevice reads 5.05, then scan one

› Message from general-purpose
  **Caveats.** I did not find docs.optitrack.com pages for the older Flex, Slim
  13E or V120/V100 cameras. I did not find an official troubleshooting page for
  a single missing camera, or an official firmware recovery procedure. Those
  parts are inference and are marked as such.

  **1. Slow blinking green (official)**
  The Camera Status Indicators page
  (docs.optitrack.com/hardware/camera-status-indicators, also v2.3/v3.1) lists
  the back ring light phases:
  - Green: "Initialize Phase 1", powered, bootloader running.
  - Yellow: Phase 2, "Firmware is running and switch communication in progress".
  - **Blinking Green (slow): Phase 3, "Switch communication established and
  awaiting an IP address."**
  - Blinking Yellow: Phase 4, fully initialized, synchronizing with the camera
  group or eSync.
  - Blinking Green (fast): "Running", fully operational and synchronized.

  The older v1.1 Prime Series page has the same wording, without the slow/fast
  distinction. The pages I read do not split the back-ring table by model, so
  Prime and PrimeX appear to share it.

  **Meaning for this case:** the camera has booted and its link to the switch is
  up, but it has no IP address or host connection. That points to DHCP or
  network configuration, not a power fault or a dead camera.

  **2. Normal connected state (official)**
  - Back ring: fast blinking green (Running).
  - Front ring: off until Motive connects and authenticates, then slow flashing
  cyan (idle), solid cyan (live), yellow (selected in Motive), solid green
  (recording).
  - The bottom-left display shows a constant camera ID assigned by Motive.

  **3 and 4. Causes, most to least likely, with five-minute checks (mostly
  inference)**
  1. **No DHCP or no host path (matches the LED).** The camera's switch is not
  uplinked to the camera network, or the port is in another VLAN or on another
  subnet.
     - Switch: link LED and activity LED on that port.
     - Motive: the camera is absent from the Devices pane.
     - Network: no IP means it stays in Phase 3. Compare with a working camera's
  port.
  2. **Bad cable or port, or a flaky data link.** PoE can power the camera while
  the data pair is faulty.
     - Switch: no link LED, or a flapping or 100 Mbps link.
     - Camera display: official docs say **E1 = failed link speed negotiation**.
     - Fix: swap the cable and port with a known-good camera.
  3. **PoE budget exceeded or reduced-power boot loop.** Check the switch's PoE
  status page. A camera repeatedly cycling through green and yellow points here.
  4. **Firmware mismatch.** Less likely, because the docs say firmware updates
  automatically when Motive starts. Watch for cyan or yellow cycling with a
  percentage on the front display. E2, E3, E4 or E5 mean corrupt firmware or
  bitstream, or a flash failure (E5: contact support).
  5. **Camera not shown or enabled in Motive.** Check the Devices pane, Motive's
  network adapter selection and the Windows firewall. Weak evidence, because
  Motive normally lists any camera that reaches it.
  6. **Sync or eSync group problem.** This would show Phase 4 (blinking yellow),
  not slow green, so it is unlikely here.
  7. **Failed camera.** Rule it out last by plugging it into a known-good port
  with a known-good cable.

  **5. Firmware update and reset**
  - Official: Motive pushes firmware automatically on connect. The ring cycles
  yellow or cyan with a percentage on the display, and alternating red means the
  flash is being reset.
  - The camera must reach Motive to be updated. I found no separate utility and
  no documented manual reset. If the camera stays stuck after the cable and port
  swap, contact NaturalPoint support (help.naturalpoint.com).

  **Sources:** https://docs.optitrack.com/hardware/camera-status-indicators and
  https://docs.optitrack.com/v1.1/hardware-setup/prime-series-status-indicators

● Agent "OptiTrack camera slow green blink" finished · 1m 6s

● Your camera's LED has an official meaning, and it's specific. From OptiTrack's
  Camera Status Indicators page, the back ring light runs through boot phases:

  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────┬───────────────────────┐
  │                      phase                      │          LED          │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────┤
  │ 1 — powered, bootloader                         │ solid green           │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────┤
  │ 2 — firmware running, switch comms in progress  │ yellow                │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────┤
  │ 3 — switch communication established, awaiting  │ slow blinking green ← │
  │ an IP address                                   │  yours                │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────┤
  │ 4 — initialised, syncing with camera            │ blinking yellow       │
  │ group/eSync                                     │                       │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────┤
  │ running, fully operational                      │ fast blinking green   │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────┴───────────────────────┘

  So the camera booted fine and its link to the switch is up — it just never got
  an IP. That rules out a dead camera, a power fault, and a sync-group problem
  (that would be blinking yellow), and points squarely at DHCP/network path. In
  five minutes, in this order:

  1. Swap cable and switch port with a known-good camera. If the fault follows
     the port, it's the port/uplink; if it follows the camera, it's the camera.
     PoE can power a camera through a cable whose data pair is bad — and the
     official docs say the camera's own display shows E1 for failed link-speed
     negotiation, so check that little display too.
  2. Check that switch is actually uplinked to the camera network and the port
     isn't in a different VLAN/subnet — "awaiting an IP" is exactly what you'd
     see if it's isolated from whatever hands out camera addresses.
  3. Check the switch's PoE budget page — a camera cycling green→yellow
     repeatedly means reduced-power boot looping.
  4. Firmware mismatch is unlikely (Motive flashes cameras automatically on
     connect, showing a percentage on the display), and E2–E5 on the display
     would indicate corrupt firmware instead.

  Normal healthy state for comparison: fast blinking green on the back ring,
  plus a camera ID on the bottom-left display.

  Two implications:

  - Nothing could fly right now even if we wanted to (we don't — nothing is
    armed).
  - The soak I just started is not a representative stall test. With zero rigid
    bodies the server sends no pose broadcasts, which in normal operation are
    ~150 packets/s — the dominant share of radio traffic. Whatever the stall
    depends on, the radio is much quieter than usual.

  That said, it's a genuinely useful control, so I've left it running: all five
  drones connected, /cfX/connection_statistics is live, and
  scripts/link_stall_recorder.py is recording to data/linkstalls/. If a drone 
  stalls even with no pose broadcasts, that eliminates broadcast load as a 
  prerequisite — which would be the first hard constraint we've had on the
  mechanism. If nothing stalls in 30+ minutes of quiet radio, that's evidence
  the load matters.

  About the missing markers: that deficiency won't show at rest — and this
  measurement is at rest. A body with too few visible markers still solves
  cleanly while stationary; what it costs you is robustness, appearing only in
  motion as brief dropouts or a yaw flip when a marker becomes occluded. Since
  CLAUDE.md's own gotcha is that a yaw error is invisible at rest and a fly-away
  in flight, that's worth testing deliberately — and it needs no flying: pick

  Read the last rows: the server is still polling cf5 ~175 times a second and 
  still receiving ~165 acks a second — while publishing nothing. That kills
  every starvation theory, including mine: cf5 is not short of slots, not
  unpolled, not RF-starved.

  What it shows instead is exactly one of the four signatures I wrote into the
  decision table: receive high, enqueued zero — packets arrive and are counted,
  then are discarded before reaching the application. And note enqueued hitting
  18446744073709551615 (2⁶⁴−1, i.e. −1 underflowed) at the precise moment of
  transition — a counter going negative is a bug signature in its own right.

  The shape also fits: a burst of loss (ack/s collapsing to ~40 for two
  seconds), then the link recovers its ack rate completely — but the payload
  path never recovers. That's the safelink down-bit mismatch the first code
  review flagged: CrazyradioThread.cpp:301-306 resets the ack to invalid
  whenever the drone's down bit disagrees with safelinkDown_, and nothing ever
  resyncs it.

  Let me confirm the localisation in code before claiming it:

      "enqueued_co…)

     src/crazyswarm2/crazyflie/deps/crazyflie_tools/crazyflie_cpp/crazyflie
     -link-cpp/src/CrazyradioThread.cpp:265:                        --con->
     … +32 lines (ctrl+o to expand)

● I have to correct myself immediately: enqueued_count is not a received-data 
  counter. It's incremented in Connection.cpp:262 when the application queues an
  outgoing packet and decremented when one is transmitted — it's an
  outgoing-queue gauge. Zero is healthy, and that 2⁶⁴−1 is the gauge
  underflowing past zero (a real accounting bug in the library, but unrelated to
  received data). My decision table in server.yaml and CLAUDE.md mislabelled
  it, and I'll fix that.

  So the question becomes where ack_count and receive_count are counted relative
  to the safelink discard:

      link-cpp/src
      grep -n "ack_count\|receive_count\|sent_count\|sent_ping_count"
      $D/Crazyra…)
