● Camera sorted, mocap verified, and the recorder caught a stall with the 
  counters — which is the first hard evidence in this whole hunt. Here's what it
  establishes, and the one correction I owe you.

  The stall, measured (data/linkstalls/stall_cf5_20261005-152134.json): cf5
  degraded over ~8 seconds (10.0 → 9.4 → 7.8 → 5.8 → 3.8 → 2.4 → 0.4 → 0 Hz)
  while cf1–cf4 stayed healthy. After it went silent, the server was still 
  sending ~175 packets/s to cf5 and still receiving ~167 valid acks/s.

  That eliminates two more theories, including the one I was most confident in:

  - Airtime/polling starvation is dead. 175 polls/s is not starvation.
  - The safelink discard is dead. That path invalidates the ack before ack_count
    is incremented (CrazyradioThread.cpp:355), so a mismatch would show
    ack_count ≈ 0. It's 167/s.

  The correction: I initially read "receive high, enqueued zero" as proof that
  payloads were being discarded. That was wrong, and it came from my own
  mislabelled decision table. Reading the code: enqueued_count is the outgoing
  queue depth (incremented when the app queues a packet, decremented when it's
  sent — Connection.cpp:260-262), so zero is healthy. And receive_count counts
  acks pushed to the app queue including the drone's 1-byte "nothing to send"
  nulls, because the null-packet filter is commented out — so receive == ack is
  the normal pattern, not evidence of real data. I've fixed both the server.yaml
  comment and the CLAUDE.md gotcha so the next session doesn't inherit my
  error.

  What the evidence now says: the drone answers every single poll, with nothing
  to send — on that link only, while a fresh connection from the second dongle
  got 10 Hz immediately. So its log blocks have stopped producing for the
  server's connection, with the radio perfectly healthy in both directions.

  The next decisive measurement, and it needs your second dongle plugged in:
  read cf5's console port while it's stalled — ros2 run crazyflie console --uri 
  radio://0/80/2M/E7E7E7E705 via the free stick. If the firmware is complaining
  (a log-subsystem error, an assert, a block overflow), that's the cause stated
  in the drone's own words. The console is the one diagnostic channel we haven't
  read all week.

  Current state: stack up, all five connected and publishing 10 Hz, mocap at 50
  Hz with zero dropouts, recorder running against data/linkstalls/. Nothing
  armed. Two things from you when convenient — plug in the 5.04 stick so I can
  read the console at the next stall, and tell me if you want the hand-carried
  marker test (powered-off drones, no flying) to check those incomplete rigid
  bodies under motion.

  - The first three dip-seconds hit all five drones simultaneously — that's just
    the recorder's startup window filling, not a rig event.

    shared, brief disturbance.

    versus 3–6 for everyone else — a 4–7× outlier.

  That cf1 asymmetry is worth noting because it keeps recurring: cf1 was the
  first drone to stall on 2 Oct, it answered only 2 of 3 scan passes, and it
  took 5.3 s for a trajectory upload where the others took 0.6–0.9 s. If
  stutters are early-stage versions of the same fault, cf1 is where it'll show
  first — which makes it the drone to watch, and a reason to keep the soak
  running rather than restart it.

  Mean rates are 9.89–9.91 Hz against a 10 Hz nominal, so nothing is
  meaningfully degraded yet. The soak continues, and the recorder will dump a
  full 60-second precursor for every drone the moment one actually goes silent —
  which is the thing we still lack.

      py/crazyflie_server.py)
  ⎿          return response

         def _poses_changed(self, msg):
     … +23 lines (ctrl+o to expand)

● Confirmed, and this explains both symptoms — cflib is the wrong tool for this 
  rig, and it's also a confounded experiment.

  Look at its mocap path: _poses_changed loops over every pose and calls
  send_extpose(...) per drone, unicast. At 50 Hz × 5 drones that's 250 unicast 
  packets/s, each wanting an ack. The C++ server instead sends mocap as
  broadcasts — sendExternalPoses packs 2 drones per packet, so ~150 unacked
  packets/s. So cflib roughly doubles the packet count and makes every one of
  them acked traffic on a single Crazyradio.

  That accounts for what you saw:

  - Dropped connections — the radio saturates, acks start failing, cflib drops
    links (it's also less tolerant: it exits or disconnects where the C++ server
    soldiers on).
  - Drones running away — if send_extpose updates are delayed or lost, the
    onboard Kalman filter has no external correction and the estimate drifts. A
    drifting estimate with locSrv.extPosStdDev = 1e-3 force-fusing position is
    precisely the documented fly-away: the controller chases a diverging
    estimate.

  So please don't arm anything under cflib — on hardware that behaviour isn't
  cosmetic, it's the runaway case. (If you genuinely meant backend:=sim, there's
  no hardware risk, but the same divergence explains the visual.)

  It's also not the clean A/B I suggested, and that's my error: cflib changes 
  two variables at once — the link stack and the mocap transport. A stall-free
  cflib run wouldn't isolate crazyflie-link-cpp, and a drop-ridden one tells us
  nothing about the C++ stall. I should have checked how it forwards mocap
  before proposing it.

  Recommendation: go back to backend:=cpp (the default — just ros2 launch 
  crazyflie launch.py), which is the only backend this rig is verified against,
  and keep the recorder running. We still have a tight box around the fault:

  - the drone logs fine to a fresh connection, flies, accepts commands, and its
    console is silent;
  - the server's own connection polls and acks normally while publishing
    nothing;
  - the link counters can't distinguish frozen from healthy, because nulls
    dominate them.

  The honest next step is no longer measurement from outside — it's
  instrumenting crazyflie_cpp's log-packet dispatch to log what happens to a log
  packet for a frozen drone: whether one ever arrives on that connection at
  all, and if it does, where it's dropped. That's a debug-print patch to
  vendored C++, built once, run until a drone freezes. It's the first thing I'd
  want your go-ahead on, because it touches the flight-critical server — though
  it only adds logging, and I'd put it behind a parameter so it's off by
  default.

  Worth also recording: cflib connects faster than cpp (seconds vs 19–35 s).
  That's a real observation about the C++ server's serial connect loop,
  unrelated to the stall but good to know.

● Opted in and skill loaded. Authoring a workflow: five parallel code readers
  over distinct lenses, each candidate then refuted against our measured facts,
  then one synthesis producing the instrumentation patch.

● Workflow(Trace where log packets are dropped for a frozen Crazyflie
          connection, then design the instrumentation patch)
  ⎿  /workflows to view dynamic workflow runs

● The workflow is running in the background on Sonnet throughout — you can watch
  it with /workflows. Its structure:

  1. Trace — five readers in parallel, each on a distinct lens: the receive path
     end-to-end; log-block dispatch and registration; who actually drives
     packet reception and whether it can stop for one drone; every latching
     per-connection field; and the null-versus-payload question (what the drone
     is answering polls with).
  2. Refute — every candidate goes to a skeptic told to default to refuted, with
     instructions to kill anything that would hit all drones, recover by
     itself, contradict the counter readings, or log a warning we never saw.
  3. Synthesise — ranked survivors, plus a concrete instrumentation patch and a
     recovery design.

  Each agent gets the full measured-facts block as ground truth — the
  healthy-looking counters, the second-dongle exoneration, zero "unrequested
  data" warnings, acked block setup, the flyable-while-frozen fact — and is told
  to quote file:line rather than theorise. I've also pre-empted the dead ends
  in the prompt, including firmware flashing, since 5.1 and 5.4 both showed it.

  While it runs, three things from the last hour worth keeping:

  - Go back to backend:=cpp when you're ready to soak again. cflib isn't viable
    here: it forwards mocap as 250 unicast acked packets/s (send_extpose per
    drone per frame) where the C++ server broadcasts ~150 unacked — that's the
    dropped connections and the diverging estimates you saw, and it was a
    confounded experiment on my part since it swaps the mocap transport as well
    as the link stack.
  - Don't arm under cflib. A delayed or dropped extpos leaves the estimator
    uncorrected, and with locSrv.extPosStdDev = 1e-3 force-fusing position, that
    is the fly-away case rather than a display artifact.
  - cflib connects in seconds where the C++ server takes 19–35 s. Unrelated to
    the stall, but a genuine observation about the C++ server's serial connect
    loop.

  I'll relay the workflow's conclusion when it lands, including which candidates
  died in refutation — that's usually the more useful half.

      src/crazyswarm2/crazyflie/src/crazyflie_server.cpp | sed -n '1,6p')
  ⎿  1015:      const auto statsUc = cf_.connectionStats();
     1021:      const auto statsBc = cfbc_->connectionStats();

● Verified: line 1077 is cf_.connectionStatsDelta(), so
  /cfX/connection_statistics publishes per-interval deltas. My per-second
  reading was right — and the 2⁶⁴−1 was a size_t subtraction artifact when the
  outgoing queue depth dropped by one between samples, exactly as the workflow
  argued. So that's not evidence of anything, and I'll stop citing it.

  The workflow's most useful contribution is a deduction, not a candidate:
  spin_once calls processAllPackets() every 1 ms (crazyflie_server.cpp:610), and
  latency replies travel the same queue → processPacket path as log data.
  on_latency keeps firing throughout a freeze. So the consumer loop is
  demonstrably alive and draining that connection — only log traffic is missing.
  That's the tightest constraint we've had.

  That leaves exactly three possibilities, each with a single observation that
  settles it:

  1. Log packets never leave the drone on this link — the trace shows nulls and
     latency replies but zero port-5/channel-2 packets for that connection.
  2. They arrive but die before dispatch — port 5/ch 2 appears at the link layer
     (CrazyradioThread.cpp:355) but never in processPacket
     (Crazyflie.cpp:1027). Note crtpLogDataResponse::valid requires port 5, ch 2
     and payload > 3 (crtp.h:540) — a malformed log packet fails valid() and is
     dropped silently, which is consistent with us seeing zero "unrequested
     data" warnings.
  3. Misdelivery to another connection — port-5 packets carrying the victim's
     block ids show up on a different connection pointer.

  The patch to tell them apart is four small insertions, all behind a ROS
  parameter defaulting to off (a dormant relaxed atomic load when disabled): a
  trace_ flag on the connection, one fprintf at the link layer after ack_count++
  skipping nulls, one at the top of processPacket printing
  port/channel/valid()/block-id-registered, and a debug.trace_cf parameter in
  the server's constructor so you launch with -p debug.trace_cf:=cf1.
  Startup-only on purpose, because this rig's same-process /parameter_events
  loopback is unreliable.

  Two caveats before I touch anything:

  - Applying it means rebuilding the crazyflie package and relaunching, which
    costs you the currently frozen cf1/cf4 state. That state can't be traced
    retroactively anyway (the flag is read at connect), and freezes recur within
    ~40 minutes, so I think it's an acceptable trade — but it's your rig and
    your session.
  - It edits vendored third-party C++ plus the flight-critical server. Off by
    default, logging only, no behaviour change — but I want your explicit
    go-ahead rather than inferring it from "investigate the packets".

  Say go and I'll apply it, build, and hand you the launch line. The recovery
  design — detect "link alive but no log data for 5 s" and rebuild that one
  drone's connection, with a hard refusal to do so while the drone might be
  airborne — I'd keep as a second step, after the trace tells us which of the
  three it is.

  │         file         │                      change                       │
  ├──────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ ConnectionImpl.h     │ std::atomic<bool> trace_{false}                   │
  ├──────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Connection.h / .cpp  │ setTrace(bool)                                    │
  ├──────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ CrazyradioThread.cpp │ at the link layer: prints every non-null ack as   │
  │                      │ addr port ch len                                  │
  ├──────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Crazyflie.h          │ m_trace + setTrace() arming both layers           │
  ├──────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Crazyflie.cpp        │ at the top of processPacket: port ch len logvalid │
  │                      │  cb                                               │
  ├──────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ crazyflie_server.cpp │ debug.trace_cf parameter, read at connect         │
  └──────────────────────┴───────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  When off it's a single relaxed atomic load per ack — no behaviour or timing
  change. Read at connect deliberately, since this rig's same-process
  /parameter_events never loop back, so a runtime toggle would silently do
  nothing.

  Safety net: every touched file was clean against HEAD (627b499), originals
  copied to data/patch-backups/trace-627b499/, with REVERT.sh offering both git 
  restore … and a copy-back path, then a rebuild. One command undoes everything.

  To run it:

  ros2 launch crazyflie launch.py --ros-args -p debug.trace_cf:=cf1
  python3 scripts/link_stall_recorder.py          # second terminal

  cf1 is the right target — it's the repeat offender (first to stall on 2 Oct,
  21 stutters vs 3–6 for the others, frozen twice today). stderr will be noisy
  for that one drone only; the others are untouched. Then when it freezes, the
  three outcomes are distinguishable:

  - No port=5 ch=2 link lines → the drone never sends log data on that link.
    Firmware-side per-link state, and the fix is server-side recovery.
  - Link lines but no host lines → lost between the receive queue and dispatch.
  - Host lines with logvalid=0 → malformed log packets, silently dropped by
    valid() — which would explain the absence of "unrequested data" warnings.
  - Host lines with logvalid=1 cb=0 → the block id isn't registered any more.

  Two honest notes on the workflow that produced this: zero of its three 
  candidates survived refutation, so this is still instrumentation rather than a
  fix. And it corrected me — the 2⁶⁴−1 I flagged as a bug signature is a benign
  size_t delta artifact (connectionStatsDelta() at crazyflie_server.cpp:1077),
  so ignore that detail; the per-second reading of the counters still stands.

  One repo-hygiene thing I noticed and did not change: data/ is not gitignored,
  so data/linkstalls/, data/arena/ and now data/patch-backups/ would be swept
  into any git add -A. I'd normally ignore it, but data/arena/*.csv are the
  measurements your safety.ARENA_RADIUS_TESTED constants cite — so a clone would
  lose that evidence. Your call: ignore the whole thing, or ignore all but
  data/arena/.

● Yes — it now traces all five at once, and that fixed a real flaw in my first 
  version. Builds clean, no new warnings.

  Two changes:

  1. The parameter takes a list or all:
  ros2 launch crazyflie launch.py --ros-args -p debug.trace_cf:=all
  # or a subset: -p debug.trace_cf:=cf1,cf3,cf5   (spaces tolerated)

  2. Tracing is now summary-based, which is what makes "all" viable. My first
  version printed per packet — about 165 lines/s per drone once nulls are
  counted, so ~825 lines/s across five, through the launch's pipe. That's enough
  I/O to perturb the timing we're measuring, and the radio thread is the worst
  place to put an fprintf. So now:

  - Link layer (radio thread): classifies each ack — log (port 5/ch 2), null
    (port 15/ch 3), other — and prints one line per second per drone: [trace 
    link E7E7E7E701] 1s: log=16 null=149 other=1
  - Host dispatcher: same idea plus the dispatch outcome: [trace host 
    radio://0/80/2M/E7E7E7E701] 1s: log=16 dispatched=16 null=149 other=0
  - Per-packet lines only for anomalies — a log-shaped packet whose block id
    isn't registered, or a port-5 packet that fails
    crtpLogDataResponse::valid(). Those are the two silent-drop paths, and
    they're exactly what wouldn't show up as an "unrequested data" warning.

  So normal operation is ~10 lines/s total for the whole fleet, and the stall
  reads directly off the summaries:

  ┌────────────────────────┬────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  what you see when a   │                   conclusion                   │
  │     drone freezes      │                                                │
  ├────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │                        │ the drone stops sending log data on that link  │
  │ link log=0 null=165    │ — firmware-side per-link state; fix is         │
  │                        │ server-side recovery                           │
  ├────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ link log=16, host      │ lost between the receive queue and the         │
  │ log=0                  │ dispatcher                                     │
  ├────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ host log=16            │                                                │
  │ dispatched=0 + ANOMALY │ block id no longer registered                  │
  │  lines                 │                                                │
  ├────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ ANOMALY "failed valid" │ malformed log packets, silently dropped        │
  │  lines                 │                                                │
  └────────────────────────┴────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  The link and host summaries are independently counted on different threads, so
  comparing them localises the loss to one side of the queue without any
  inference.

  Revert is unchanged: data/patch-backups/trace-627b499/REVERT.sh.

  Launch it with debug.trace_cf:=all plus the recorder, and the next freeze —
  whichever drone it picks — should name its own cause.

● Found the trigger. Look at cf5's last two healthy seconds:

  line 1160:  [trace link e7e7e7e705] 1s: log=18 null=93  other=2     ← normal
  line 1183:  [trace link e7e7e7e705] 1s: log=50 null=1   other=2     ← BURST
              [WARN] [cf5] last latency update: 1.909 s
              [WARN] [cf5] last latency update: 2.909 s

  The sequence is: cf5's link goes quiet for ~3 s (its latency echo stops), then
  it flushes a backlog — 50 log packets in one second, nulls collapsing to 1 —
  and after that burst it produces zero log packets, forever.

  So the drone queues log data during a link disturbance, dumps the backlog when
  the link recovers, and then its log blocks stop producing entirely. That
  matches every earlier observation: the drone still answers polls and commands,
  a fresh connection gets 10 Hz immediately, nothing is dropped host-side, no
  firmware console complaint, and only a server restart (which recreates the
  blocks) fixes it.

● Careful — that kills the neat story: cf1 had log=34 null=0, a fully saturated 
  burst, and survived, and cf3/cf4 both returned cleanly to log=16 after bursts
  of 48 and 54. So a backlog flush is common and usually harmless; cf5's freeze
  followed one, but the flush isn't sufficient to cause it. I won't claim it as
  the cause.

  What is now established, by direct measurement on two independently-counted
  layers:

  At the freeze, zero log packets leave the drone on that connection. Not
  dropped, not misdispatched, not failing validation — never sent. Host and link
  agree (log=0 dispatched=0, ~200 nulls/s). That eliminates the two host-side
  candidates outright, and the fault sits in the drone's per-link log
  production: it answers every poll with "nothing to send" while a fresh
  connection to the same drone streams 10 Hz.

  Two corrections to things I said earlier:

  - The anomaly lines are my detector's fault, not a bug. All 48 are at connect,
    8 per drone, and they're port-5 control responses — log TOC entries and
    block create/start acks on channels 0/1 — which legitimately fail
    crtpLogDataResponse::valid since that accepts only channel 2. I should have
    excluded those channels.
  - The ~3 s latency gap and burst preceding cf5's freeze are context, not 
    cause, given cf1 survived an identical saturated burst.

  The important consequence: we no longer need the exact firmware trigger to fix
  this. The signature is unambiguous and machine-detectable — link alive, zero 
  log packets, for N seconds — and the remedy is known to work, because a server
  restart fixes it by recreating the blocks. So the fix is a per-drone recovery
  that does what a restart does, without the restart:

  1. Detect: stamp the last log-data arrival per drone; trigger when it's older
     than ~5 s while latency echoes are still current (< 2 s).
  2. Tier 1: stop and recreate that drone's log blocks in place.
  3. Tier 2, if tier 1 doesn't revive it within ~3 s: tear down and rebuild that
     one Crazyflie connection.
  4. Never while it might be airborne — and telemetry is dead precisely when
     we'd want to check, so the guard has to be "any
     takeoff/goTo/trajectory/cmdFullState issued since the last confirmed
     landing, or mocap z > 0.1 m" → alarm only, no rebuild.

  We also have a frozen cf5 sitting there right now, which is the ideal test
  subject for tier 1.

  Shall I implement that? Same safety net as before (everything's backed up,
  REVERT.sh in place), and I'd fold in the anomaly-detector fix. It's a real
  behaviour change in the flight-critical server rather than logging, so I want
  your explicit go-ahead — including on the airborne guard, since that's the
  part where getting it wrong matters.

✻ Sautéed for 2m 48s · done 4:21 PM

❯ You can try it out. But more importantly, why does the plan even need the     
  poses estimate to stream to fly if we are relying on the mocaps?              

● Good question, and it exposes something real about my gate rather than about
  the rig.

  You're right that flight doesn't need it. Control flows to the drone: the
  server broadcasts mocap poses (sendExternalPoses), the firmware fuses them
  into its Kalman filter, and the onboard controller flies. /cfX/pose is the
  drone's estimate coming back — pure telemetry. That's exactly why you could
  arm, take off and land a frozen cf1: the control path was never broken.

  What the gate actually buys is the only proof that the last link in the chain 
  works. Everything up to the drone is observable from the host — Motive
  streaming, /poses at 50 Hz, the server broadcasting. But "did the drone
  receive and fuse those poses?" is observable only through its own estimate
  coming back. If it hadn't — wrong address, estimator misconfigured, locSrv
  params not applied — the drone would sit there with a free-running estimate
  looking perfectly fine on the floor, and dart the instant it lifted. Agreement
  between /cfX/pose and the mocap mark is the end-to-end confirmation that
  fusion is live.

  It's also the only way to catch the yaw case in CLAUDE.md: a rotated Motive
  rigid body still gives ~1 mm position error at rest because extPosStdDev = 
  1e-3 force-fuses position, so the error is invisible until it flies. The
  preflight GUI's err.yaw compares mocap yaw against the onboard yaw — no
  telemetry, no check.

  So the gate is justified, but it's too blunt in one specific way, and your
  situation is the proof: it treats "never confirmed" the same as "confirmed
  healthy 60 seconds ago, then the telemetry link died". Those are very
  different risks. If a drone was publishing 10 Hz during the placement check
  and only froze afterwards, its fusion was verified and nothing about the
  control path changed.

  So I'll make the gate distinguish them:

  - never seen a pose → hard refusal, as now (that's the fly-away case),
  - seen healthy earlier this session, now stale → loud warning naming the drone
    and how long ago it was confirmed, and it proceeds only with an explicit
    opt-in parameter.

  That keeps the real protection while stopping the rig's telemetry bug from
  grounding a verified-safe fleet.

  Now implementing the recovery you approved — detector, tier 1, the airborne
  guard, plus the anomaly-detector fix:
